<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For some even <i>n</i> ≥ 8 and integer <i>k</i> ≥ 2, we construct a complete smooth metric on ℝ<sup><i>n</i></sup> with nonnegative Ricci curvature whose space of real harmonic functions of pointwise polynomial growth at most <i>k</i> has dimension larger than the Euclidean harmonic-polynomial dimension. The metric is Euclidean near the origin, has asymptotic volume ratio strictly between zero and one, and has nonunique tangent cones at infinity. This answers the integer-degree form of Yau's dimension comparison question in the negative.

- **claim-main-counterexample** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There are an even integer $n\geq8$, an integer $k\geq2$, and a complete smooth metric $g$ on $\mathbb R^n$ such that $\operatorname{Ric}_g\geq0$ and $$h_k(\mathbb R^n,g)>h_k(\mathbb R^n,g_{\mathrm E}).$$ The metric is Euclidean near the origin, and its asymptotic volume ratio satisfies $$0<\lim_{R\to\infty} \frac{\operatorname{vol}_g B_g(0,R)}{\omega_nR^n}<1,$$ where $\omega_n$ is the volume of the Euclidean unit ball.
  - Explanation: There is a complete smooth metric on Euclidean space with nonnegative Ricci curvature that is Euclidean near the origin and has asymptotic volume ratio strictly between zero and one. At an integer degree it has strictly more polynomial-growth harmonic functions than Euclidean space.
- **claim-berger-link-formulas** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-berger-metrics)): The volume form of $G(a,q,J)$ is $a^m\,d\operatorname{vol}_{g_0}$. Its Ricci endomorphism has eigenvalues $$a^{-2}q^{1/m}\bigl((m-1)-2(q-1)\bigr) \quad\hbox{horizontally},\qquad a^{-2}q^{1/m}(m-1)q \quad\hbox{vertically}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:link-margin} \operatorname{Ric}_{G(a,q,J)}>(m-1)G(a,q,J) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a^2<q^{1/m} \min\left\{1-\frac{2(q-1)}{m-1},\,q\right\}.$$ Every $V_l$ is preserved by the positive Laplacian of $G(a,q,J)$, whose restriction is $$\label{eq:angular-operator} A_l(a,q,J) =a^{-2}q^{1/m} \left[B_lI+(q^{-1}-1)(-D_J^2\|_{V_l})\right].$$
  - Explanation: The volume-normalized Berger metric has a constant multiple of the round volume form. Its Ricci eigenvalues are given horizontally and vertically, which yields a Ricci margin criterion. Every space of round harmonics of fixed degree is preserved by its angular Laplacian.
- **claim-hopf-weight-distribution** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hopf-distribution)): For $m=2s-1$ and $0\le b\le l$, the eigenvalue $(2b-l)^2$ of $-D_{J_0}^2$ on $V_l$ has the following contribution to its multiplicity: $$\begin{aligned} h_{l,b} &=\binom{s+b-1}{s-1}\binom{s+l-b-1}{s-1} -\binom{s+b-2}{s-1}\binom{s+l-b-2}{s-1} \label{eq:hopf-multiplicity}\\ &=\frac{l+s-1}{s-1} \binom{b+s-2}{s-2}\binom{l-b+s-2}{s-2}.\notag \end{aligned}$$ Terms with a missing bidegree in the first line are zero. With probabilities $h_{l,b}/N_l$, the quantities $(2b-l)^2/l^2$ converge in distribution, as $l\to\infty$, to $$Y_m\sim\operatorname{Beta}\left(\frac12,\frac{m-1}{2}\right).$$
  - Explanation: The multiplicity of each Hopf eigenvalue on the harmonics of a given degree is computed exactly by binomial formulas. The rescaled squared charges converge in distribution to a Beta law.
- **claim-weighted-quantile-gap** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-weighted-quantile)): For all sufficiently large odd $m$, $$\label{eq:weighted-quantile-gap} (m-1)\mathcal J_m>2.$$
  - Explanation: For large odd link dimension the weighted quantile integral of the limiting Beta law, scaled by the link dimension minus one, exceeds the threshold coming from the horizontal curvature factor.
- **claim-spectral-surplus** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-spectral-surplus)): There is an odd $m=2s-1\ge7$ for which the following choices are possible. The parameters $a_*\in(0,1)$ and $q_*>1$ can be taken arbitrarily close to $1$, with <a href="#eq:link-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-margin">[eq:link-margin]</a> holding at $a=a_*$ for every $q\in[1,q_*]$. For these parameters there are integers $k\ge2$, $L\ge2$, and $0\le M_l\le N_l$ for $2\le l\le L$ such that, on abbreviating $d_{l,i}=d_{l,i}(a_*,q_*)$, $$\label{eq:selection} \overline d_l:=\frac1{M_l}\sum_{i=1}^{M_l}d_{l,i}<k \quad\text{whenever }M_l>0, \qquad \sum_{l=2}^L M_l>\sum_{l=0}^k N_l.$$
  - Explanation: Link parameters, an integer degree, a finite degree range and block sizes can be chosen so that the mean growth rate in each selected block stays below the integer while the total number of selected directions beats the Euclidean count.
- **claim-simultaneous-control** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-control)): Fix positive numbers $\kappa_l$, $2\leq l\leq L$. As $J$ ranges over all orthogonal complex structures on $\mathbb R^{2s}$, the tuples $$X_J=\bigl(\kappa_l(D_J^2\|_{V_l})_{\mathrm{tf}}\bigr)_{l=2}^L \label{eq:control-generators}$$ generate, under real linear combinations and brackets, the full Lie algebra $$\bigoplus_{l=2}^L\mathfrak{sl}(V_l).$$
  - Explanation: Trace-free squares of the Hopf generators, taken over all orthogonal complex structures, generate the full direct sum of special linear Lie algebras over the selected degrees under real combinations and brackets.
- **claim-finite-word-invertible-differential** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-word)): Let $d=\sum_{l=2}^L(N_l^2-1)$. There exist finitely many orthogonal complex structures $J_1,\ldots,J_R$, an open neighborhood $U$ of zero in $\mathbb R^d$, and smooth real functions $c_1,\ldots,c_R$ on $U$ such that $$\mathcal W(h)= \exp(c_R(h)X_{J_R})\cdots\exp(c_1(h)X_{J_1})$$ satisfies $\mathcal W(0)=\Pi$, and $$D\mathcal W(0):\mathbb R^d\longrightarrow T_\Pi\mathcal G$$ is an isomorphism. The same assertion holds for any prescribed target in $\mathcal G$.
  - Explanation: A finite product of exponentials of the generators, with smooth real times, realizes a prescribed target in the product group, and its differential at the base parameter is invertible.
- **claim-complete-metric-ricci** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-metric)): For the fixed spectral data above, there are a smooth function $a:\mathbb R\to(0,1]$ and an index $j_{\rm geom}$ such that, for every $j_0\geq j_{\rm geom}$, every sequence $(h_j)_{j\geq j_0}$ in $K$, used in the preceding scheme, defines a smooth complete metric on $\mathbb R^{m+1}$ with nonnegative Ricci curvature: $$g=dr^2+r^2\gamma(\log r),\qquad \gamma(t)=G(a(t),q(t),J(t)).$$ The function $a$ is identically $1$ for $t\leq2$, decreases to $a_*$, and, for some $\mu>0$, satisfies $$\label{eq:scale-tail} b(t):=\frac{a'(t)}{a(t)} =-\mu(1+t)^{-5/4}\quad(t\geq4), \qquad \log\frac{a(t)}{a_*}=4\mu(1+t)^{-1/4}\quad(t\geq4).$$ The metric is Euclidean near the origin, and its distance from the origin is exactly $r$. Its volume form is $$d\operatorname{vol}_g =r^m a(\log r)^m\,dr\,d\operatorname{vol}_{g_0}.$$ The function $a$ and the starting index $j_0$ can be chosen independently of the sequence of control parameters.
  - Explanation: The five-part period schedule, with a suitable radial scale function, defines a smooth complete metric on Euclidean space with nonnegative Ricci curvature for every admitted sequence of control parameters. The metric is Euclidean near the origin.
- **claim-damped-matrix-difference** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-damping)): Let $E:[u,v]\to\mathbb R^{N\times N}$ be continuously differentiable, and let $F,\mathsf L,\mathsf R$ be continuous matrix-valued functions on the same interval. Suppose $$E'=F-\mathsf L E-E\mathsf R$$ there, where $\mathsf L,\mathsf R$ are symmetric and $\lambda_{\min}(\mathsf L)+\lambda_{\min}(\mathsf R)\geq\gamma>0$. Then, for $u\leq t\leq v$, $$\begin{aligned} \\|E(t)\\|&\leq e^{-\gamma(t-u)}\\|E(u)\\| +\int_u^t e^{-\gamma(t-w)}\\|F(w)\\|\,dw, \label{eq:damping-pointwise}\\ \int_u^v\\|E(t)\\|\,dt &\leq\gamma^{-1} \left(\\|E(u)\\|+\int_u^v\\|F(t)\\|\,dt\right). \label{eq:damping-integrated} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: A matrix difference equation with symmetric, uniformly positive damping terms satisfies a pointwise exponential estimate and an integrated estimate, without requiring commuting matrices.
- **claim-center-regular-riccati** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-regular)): There are a threshold $j_{\rm reg}\geq j_{\rm geom}$ and constants $0<c_P<C_P$ such that the following holds for every $j_0\geq j_{\rm reg}$, every sequence of parameters in $K$, and every $2\leq l\leq L$. There is a unique smooth symmetric matrix $P_l(t)$ satisfying $$P_l'=A_l-pP_l-P_l^2,\qquad P_l=lI\quad(t\leq2). \label{eq:riccati}$$ It satisfies $$c_PI\leq P_l(t)\leq C_PI \qquad(t\in\mathbb R,\ 2\leq l\leq L). \label{eq:riccati-barriers}$$ The solutions of $Y'=P_lY$ are exactly the center-regular solutions of <a href="#eq:harmonic-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:harmonic-ode">[eq:harmonic-ode]</a>. Their value vector at any one time can be prescribed arbitrarily and determines the solution uniquely. At each pulse start one also has $$\\|P_l(t_j)-\theta_l I\\|\leq Cj^{-2} \qquad(j\geq j_0), \label{eq:round-reset}$$ including the first pulse. The constants and the threshold are uniform over all preceding choices of the parameters in $K$.
  - Explanation: Center-regular solutions of the radial harmonic equation are exactly the solutions of a first-order system driven by a unique symmetric Riccati solution. That solution stays between positive barriers and returns close to a scalar matrix at each pulse start.
- **claim-pulse-limit** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pulse)): For the fixed compact ball and pulse family, $$\begin{aligned} e^{-\varphi_{l,j}}\mathcal B_{l,j}(h) &=H_l(1,h)+O_{C^1}(\eta_j),\\ P_{l,e}(h)&=\theta_lI+O_{C^1}(\eta_j). \end{aligned} \label{eq:pulse-limit}$$ Here $O_{C^1}(\eta_j)$ means that the norm and the norm of its first derivative in the current parameter $h$, uniformly on $K$, are at most $C\eta_j$. The constants and the large-$j$ threshold are independent of every admitted preceding history.
  - Explanation: Over one pulse, the normalized value transmission and the exit derivative match the ideal control target up to an error that decays with the period index, together with one parameter derivative.
- **claim-leg-normalization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-leg)): The matrices $U_{l,j}$, $V_{l,j}$, and $D_{l,j}$ have positive diagonal entries, and $$0\leq K_{l,j}\leq\theta_l^{-1}I. \label{eq:K-bound}$$ They depend only on the prescribed diagonal leg, not on its current or earlier pulse parameters. The exact transmission identity is $$D_{l,j}^{-1}\mathcal T_{l,j}(h) =\left[I+K_{l,j}\bigl(P_{l,e}(h)-\theta_lI\bigr)\right] \mathcal B_{l,j}(h). \label{eq:full-normalization}$$ Moreover, uniformly for the finite set of $l,i$, $$\log (D_{l,j})_{ii} =L_jd_{l,i}+O(1+L_jj^{-2}+T_j) =L_j\bigl(d_{l,i}+o(1)\bigr). \label{eq:diagonal-growth}$$
  - Explanation: Propagation over the diagonal leg factors exactly into a positive diagonal growth matrix times an error factor bounded independently of the leg length, and the diagonal growth has the stated logarithmic asymptotics.
- **claim-exact-period-transmission** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-transmission)): For finite spectral data as specified at the end of Section <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">3</a>, choose the word and compact pulse family above. There are a starting index $j_0$ and parameters $h_j\in K$, for every $j\geq j_0$, such that the center-regular value transmissions of the resulting metric satisfy $$\mathcal T_{l,j}=s_{l,j}D_{l,j}\Pi_l,\qquad s_{l,j}>0,\qquad \|\log s_{l,j}\|\leq C T_j \quad(2\leq l\leq L,\ j\geq j_0). \label{eq:exact-transmission}$$ The positive diagonal matrices $D_{l,j}$ propagate the reference Cauchy data $(I,\theta_l I)$ over the entire leg following the pulse; they satisfy <a href="#eq:diagonal-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagonal-growth">[eq:diagonal-growth]</a>. The regular value equation has the bounds <a href="#eq:riccati-barriers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:riccati-barriers">[eq:riccati-barriers]</a>. All estimates used to choose $j_0$ are uniform over preceding admitted controls for this fixed finite family.
  - Explanation: A contraction argument tunes the control parameters in every period so that the full-period transmission equals a positive scalar times the diagonal growth matrix times the target signed cycle.
- **claim-cone-geometry** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-cone-consequences)): Let $g$ be the metric constructed in the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, with link parameters $a_*,q_*$ and comparison integer $k$. Every cone with link $G(a_*,q,J_0)$, $1\leq q\leq q_*$, occurs as a pointed tangent cone at infinity. In particular the tangent cone is not unique. The metric $g$ is not locally conformally flat. If $\mathcal D(M)$ denotes the union of nonnegative homogeneous harmonic degrees over all its tangent cones, then $\mathcal D(M)$ contains a half-line and a neighborhood of $k$.
  - Explanation: The constructed metric has a continuum of tangent cones at infinity, is not locally conformally flat, and its union of cone degrees contains a half-line and a neighborhood of the chosen integer.

        "exact_quote": "The volume form of $G(a,q,J)$ is $a^m\\,d\\operatorname{vol}_{g_0}$.         "source_id": "PM-361.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-hopf-distribution",
        "exact_quote": "For $m=2s-1$ and $0\\le b\\le l$, the eigenvalue $(2b-l)^2$ of $-D_{J_0}^2$ on $V_l$ has the following contribution to its multiplicity: $$\\begin{aligned}\n h_{l,b}\n &=\\binom{s+b-1}{s-1}\\binom{s+l-b-1}{s-1}\n   -\\binom{s+b-2}{s-1}\\binom{s+l-b-2}{s-1} \\label{eq:hopf-multiplicity}\\\\\n &=\\frac{l+s-1}{s-1}\n   \\binom{b+s-2}{s-2}\\binom{l-b+s-2}{s-2}.\\notag\n\\end{aligned}$$ Terms with a missing bidegree in the first line are zero.         "source_id": "PM-361.1"

        "exact_quote": "For the fixed spectral data above, there are a smooth function $a:\\mathbb R\\to(0,1]$ and an index $j_{\\rm geom}$ such that, for every $j_0\\geq j_{\\rm geom}$, every sequence $(h_j)_{j\\geq j_0}$ in $K$, used in the preceding scheme, defines a smooth complete metric on $\\mathbb R^{m+1}$ with nonnegative Ricci curvature: $$g=dr^2+r^2\\gamma(\\log r),\\qquad\n \\gamma(t)=G(a(t),q(t),J(t)).$$ The function $a$ is identically $1$ for $t\\leq2$, decreases to $a_*$, and, for some $\\mu>0$, satisfies $$\\label{eq:scale-tail}\n b(t):=\\frac{a'(t)}{a(t)}\n       =-\\mu(1+t)^{-5/4}\\quad(t\\geq4),\n \\qquad\n \\log\\frac{a(t)}{a_*}=4\\mu(1+t)^{-1/4}\\quad(t\\geq4).$$ The metric is Euclidean near the origin, and its distance from the origin is exactly $r$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-damping",
        "exact_quote": "Let $E:[u,v]\\to\\mathbb R^{N\\times N}$ be continuously differentiable, and let $F,\\mathsf L,\\mathsf R$ be continuous matrix-valued functions on the same interval. Suppose $$E'=F-\\mathsf L E-E\\mathsf R$$ there, where $\\mathsf L,\\mathsf R$ are symmetric and $\\lambda_{\\min}(\\mathsf L)+\\lambda_{\\min}(\\mathsf R)\\geq\\gamma>0$. Then, for $u\\leq t\\leq v$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|E(t)\\|&\\leq e^{-\\gamma(t-u)}\\|E(u)\\|\n       +\\int_u^t e^{-\\gamma(t-w)}\\|F(w)\\|\\,dw,\n       \\label{eq:damping-pointwise}\\\\\n \\int_u^v\\|E(t)\\|\\,dt\n &\\leq\\gamma^{-1}\n       \\left(\\|E(u)\\|+\\int_u^v\\|F(t)\\|\\,dt\\right).\n       \\label{eq:damping-integrated}\n\\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-361.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-damping",
          "exact_quote": "Let $E:[u,v]\\to\\mathbb R^{N\\times N}$ be continuously differentiable, and let $F,\\mathsf L,\\mathsf R$ be continuous matrix-valued functions on the same interval. Suppose $$E'=F-\\mathsf L E-E\\mathsf R$$ there, where $\\mathsf L,\\mathsf R$ are symmetric and $\\lambda_{\\min}(\\mathsf L)+\\lambda_{\\min}(\\mathsf R)\\geq\\gamma>0$. Then, for $u\\leq t\\leq v$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|E(t)\\|&\\leq e^{-\\gamma(t-u)}\\|E(u)\\|\n       +\\int_u^t e^{-\\gamma(t-w)}\\|F(w)\\|\\,dw,\n       \\label{eq:damping-pointwise}\\\\\n \\int_u^v\\|E(t)\\|\\,dt\n &\\leq\\gamma^{-1}\n       \\left(\\|E(u)\\|+\\int_u^v\\|F(t)\\|\\,dt\\right).\n       \\label{eq:damping-integrated}\n\\end{aligned}$$",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Harmonic growth space and its dimension** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Write $h_d(M,g)=\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathcal H_d(M,g)$.
- **Orthogonal complex structure** ([source](#sec:links)): An orthogonal complex structure is a real linear map $J$ satisfying $J^2=-I$ and $J^{\mathsf T}J=I$.
- **Center-regular function** ([source](#pm-h-12)): The function $u$ is called *center-regular* if it extends smoothly across the origin.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-berger-links** [claim](#stmt-lem-berger-metrics) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-berger-metrics): Direct curvature computation by connection change and Koszul formula, then divergence computation for the Laplacian. -> Volume form, Ricci eigenvalues, margin criterion, angular operator on round harmonics.
- **step-hopf-distribution** [claim](#stmt-lem-hopf-distribution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hopf-distribution): Bidegree decomposition of complexified harmonics, dimension count, beta-binomial representation. -> Exact multiplicities and Beta limit of squared charges.
- **step-weighted-quantile** [claim](#stmt-lem-weighted-quantile) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-weighted-quantile): Normal limit of the Beta law, Fatou's lemma, Mills bound and Jensen's inequality. -> Strict gap for large odd link dimension.
- **step-spectral-surplus** [claim](#stmt-lem-spectral-surplus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spectral-surplus): Choose link parameters, expand limiting means to first order, count initial groups by a layer-cake formula and apply Fatou. -> Integer degree, degree range and block sizes with strict surplus.
- **step-lie-control** [claim](#stmt-prop-control) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-control): Weight-space analysis, rank-one symmetric operator, spanning by round orbit, Goursat-type argument for independence of degrees. -> Generation of the direct sum of special linear Lie algebras.
- **step-finite-word** [claim](#stmt-prop-word) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-word): Span of adjoint orbits, inverse function theorem, connectedness of the group. -> Finite word of exponentials with invertible differential at the target.
- **step-metric** [claim](#stmt-prop-metric) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-metric): Radial scale from a cutoff, shape endomorphism, Codazzi and Gauss formulas, uniform positivity of Ricci components on periods. -> Smooth complete metric with nonnegative Ricci curvature for every admitted control sequence.
- **step-damping** [claim](#stmt-lem-damping) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-damping): Frobenius inner product differential inequality and integration. -> Exponential and integrated estimates for damped matrix differences.
- **step-regular-riccati** [claim](#stmt-lem-regular) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-regular): Barrier argument for the Riccati equation, fundamental matrix, damped reset estimate. -> Bounded symmetric Riccati solution and reset near a scalar at pulse starts.
- **step-pulse** [claim](#stmt-lem-pulse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pulse): Scalar baseline solution, Riccati residual, damped error with parameter derivative, Gronwall-type comparison. -> Pulse transmission close to the ideal word in C^1.
- **step-leg** [claim](#stmt-lem-leg) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-leg): Diagonal scalar equations, positivity, exact factorization, logarithmic derivative estimate. -> Exact normalization of the leg and dwell growth.
- **step-transmission** [claim](#stmt-prop-transmission) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-transmission): Determinant normalization, contraction mapping inversion of the word map, induction over periods. -> Exact full-period transmissions equal to scalar times diagonal times signed cycle.
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-transmission-2): Cycle sums of logarithmic growth, extension to every radius, linear independence, volume limit. -> Strict dimension surplus with intermediate asymptotic volume ratio.
- **step-cone-geometry** [claim](#stmt-prop-cone-consequences) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cone-consequences): Annular limits, Weyl tensor formula on the product, continuity of growth roots in the squashing parameter. -> Nonunique cones, failure of conformal flatness, cone degrees around the integer.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-euclidean-count-identity**: For a link dimension m and integer degree k, the sum over l from 0 to k of the harmonic-polynomial dimensions N_l equals the closed-form Euclidean comparator for ambient dimension m+1, so the finite count that the surplus argument compares against is exactly the Euclidean harmonic-polynomial dimension.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-linear-degree**: Using the comparison failure to claim a surplus of linear-growth harmonic functions. -> The counterexample needs integer degree at least two; at degree one the sharp Euclidean bound holds under nonnegative Ricci curvature.
- **anti-arithmetic-not-metric**: Treating the exact integer counting program as a construction or numerical certificate of the metric itself. -> The program only checks finite spectral and dimension arithmetic; it says nothing about the control word, transmission or curvature of the metric.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The paper's finite integer calculation compares a selected dimension with the Euclidean count; this panel checks the finite exact identity that the sum of the harmonic-polynomial dimensions N_l up to degree k equals the closed-form Euclidean comparator.

```python
# INPUTS: one line with two integers m and k (link dimension m>=1, degree k>=1), read from stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: m>=1 and k>=1 integers; ambient dimension is m+1.
# OUTPUTS: two integers on one line: sum over l=0..k of N_l, and the closed form comb(m+k,k)+comb(m+k-1,k-1).
# TIME: O(k) big-integer binomial evaluations.
# SPACE: O(1) big integers beyond the output.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, no floating point.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and a nonzero exit status.
import sys
from math import comb
m, k = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
def N(l):
    return comb(m+l,m) - (comb(m+l-2,m) if l>=2 else 0)
total = sum(N(l) for l in range(k+1))
closed = comb(m+k,k) + (comb(m+k-1,k-1) if k>=1 else 0)
print(total, closed)
```

| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existence theorem about a metric on Euclidean space. The finite arithmetic selection is a certificate check, and the paper states it builds neither the control word nor the metric numerically, so no software component follows. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The radial harmonic equation and its matrix Riccati flow are used only as proof devices in an asymptotic regime. They are not a simulation scheme with stated step sizes or error control. |

| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main proofs are existential, built on asymptotics, Lie-algebra generation and inverse-function arguments with unspecified thresholds. The exact-integer selection is explicitly separate from the analytic argument, so no algorithm with a stated cost is delivered. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only combinatorial objects are multiplicity formulas for Hopf weights on spherical harmonics. These are counting identities, not a storage or indexing structure. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns computation on hardware. The relevant statements are about a smooth Riemannian metric that is Euclidean near the origin and has a regular harmonic solution space. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The weighted quantile inequality for a Beta-type law is a purely analytic fact about large odd link dimension. It yields no hardness assumption, primitive or security reduction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper answers a question in geometric analysis about the exact Euclidean count at an integer degree. It contains no learning method, and any link to manifold-learning benchmarks would be speculative. |

| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The nonuniqueness of tangent cones at infinity is a geometric statement about limits of rescaled metrics. It has no connection to nodes, messages or consensus. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-pointwise-growth** (formal_scope): The harmonic growth space is defined by a pointwise bound at every distance, so a bound along a sequence of spheres, or a growth average along a sequence of radii, does not by itself place a function in the space. Blocked use: ['Counting harmonic functions from growth estimates along a sequence of radii alone.', 'Treating a logarithmic growth average along a sequence of radii as a pointwise bound.']
- **limit-degree-and-dimension** (hypothesis): The theorem is an existence statement: it provides some even ambient dimension at least eight and some integer degree at least two. It does not assert a surplus for every dimension or every degree. Blocked use: ['Claiming a counterexample in a prescribed low dimension.', 'Claiming a counterexample at degree one.']
- **limit-large-odd-link-dimension** (nonconstructive): The weighted quantile inequality is proved only for all sufficiently large odd link dimension, through limits and Fatou's lemma, so the threshold is not explicit in the analytic argument. Blocked use: ['Using the inequality at a specific small odd link dimension without a separate check.', 'Reading an effective bound on the link dimension off the analytic proof.']
- **limit-asymptotic-surplus** (nonconstructive): The strict surplus in the spectral count is obtained for every sufficiently large integer degree by a liminf argument, with no explicit degree supplied by the analytic proof. Blocked use: ['Extracting a concrete degree or degree range from the analytic existence argument.', 'Assuming the surplus holds at every integer degree.']
- **limit-finite-selection-scope** (formal_scope): The exact integer selection at specific parameters verifies only the finite spectral inequalities. The paper states that it neither constructs the control word nor the metric numerically, and that the analytic argument is independent of it. Blocked use: ['Presenting the integer program as a numerical certificate of the metric, its curvature or its transmission matrices.', 'Using the integer program in place of the analytic control and transmission arguments.']
- **limit-all-complex-structures** (hypothesis): The Lie-algebra generation statement quantifies over all orthogonal complex structures, both orientation classes included, and the averaging step uses both. Restricting to one class can invalidate that step. Blocked use: ['Restricting the control family to a single orientation class of complex structures.', 'Applying the generation result to a proper subfamily of structures.']
- **limit-starting-index-late** (edge_case): Curvature positivity, the Riccati barriers and the contraction tuning hold only after a sufficiently large starting period index. Large bounds for a fixed finite pulse family are absorbed by increasing that index. Blocked use: ['Asserting nonnegative Ricci curvature in the early periods of the schedule.', 'Changing the pulse family or parameter ball after the starting index has been fixed.']
- **limit-fixed-finite-data** (hypothesis): All constants depend on the fixed finite degree range and the fixed compact family of controls, and are uniform only in the period index and in the preceding admissible controls. Blocked use: ['Enlarging the degree range or the control family while keeping the same constants and threshold.', 'Using the estimates for infinitely many harmonic degrees at once.']
- **limit-not-conformally-flat** (counterexample_scope): The constructed metric is not locally conformally flat, so it lies outside the setting where a sharp Euclidean comparison is known, and its cone-degree union contains a neighborhood of the chosen integer, which blocks the three-circles argument there. Blocked use: ['Using the counterexample against results that assume local conformal flatness.', 'Applying the nonunique-cone three-circles theorem at the chosen integer degree or nearby exponents.']
- **limit-hopf-boundary-terms** (edge_case): The Hopf multiplicity formulas treat missing bidegrees as zero, and contributions of paired charges are combined except for the middle one, so counts at boundary charges and parity cases need this convention. Blocked use: ['Applying the multiplicity formula without the zero convention for missing bidegrees.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 361 (`lean/docs/361.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A counterexample to integer-degree harmonic dimension comparison](../../preprints/A-counterexample-to-integer-degree-harmonic-dimension-comparison-September-25-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized result disproves the proposed Euclidean upper comparison for dimensions of harmonic functions with integer polynomial growth. It constructs one complete smooth metric with nonnegative Ricci curvature on an even-dimensional Euclidean space, Euclidean near the origin and with asymptotic volume ratio strictly between zero and one, admitting more independent harmonic functions of the prescribed growth degree than the Euclidean count. The construction uses dimension $16$ and degree $50000$. Separate tangent-cone and cone-spectrum conclusions are not included.

Comparator source `HarmonicGrowth.lean` (4511 bytes, sha256 `02c9e5d8654bbbd6cc4de899fcaaf1fa550252cb0b3f3418921ffb8711f263fb`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open Filter MeasureTheory
open scoped BigOperators Topology ENNReal ContDiff

namespace HarmonicCounterexample

abbrev Space (n : ℕ) := Fin n → ℝ
abbrev Mat (n : ℕ) := Matrix (Fin n) (Fin n) ℝ

/-- A genuine smooth positive definite covariant metric tensor on R^n. -/
structure SmoothMetric (n : ℕ) where
  coeff : Space n → Mat n
  smooth : ∀ i j, ContDiff ℝ ∞ (fun x => coeff x i j)
  positive : ∀ x, (coeff x).PosDef

/-- The coordinate directional derivative. -/
def coordDeriv {n : ℕ} (i : Fin n) (f : Space n → ℝ) (x : Space n) : ℝ :=
  fderiv ℝ f x (Pi.single i 1)

/-- Levi-Civita connection in the single global Cartesian chart. -/
def christoffel {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (x : Space n)
    (k i j : Fin n) : ℝ :=
  (1 / 2 : ℝ) * ∑ l, (g.coeff x)⁻¹ k l *
    (coordDeriv i (fun y => g.coeff y j l) x +
     coordDeriv j (fun y => g.coeff y i l) x -
     coordDeriv l (fun y => g.coeff y i j) x)

/-- Ricci tensor, with the convention in which the round sphere has positive Ricci. -/
def ricci {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (x : Space n) (i j : Fin n) : ℝ :=
  (∑ k, (coordDeriv k (fun y => christoffel g y k i j) x -
    coordDeriv j (fun y => christoffel g y k i k) x)) +
  ∑ k, ∑ l, (christoffel g x k i j * christoffel g x l k l -
    christoffel g x l i k * christoffel g x k j l)

def RicciNonnegative {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) : Prop :=
  ∀ (x v : Space n), 0 ≤ ∑ i, ∑ j, ricci g x i j * v i * v j

/-- Trace of the covariant Hessian, i.e. the Laplace-Beltrami operator. -/
def laplaceBeltrami {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (u : Space n → ℝ)
    (x : Space n) : ℝ :=
  ∑ i, ∑ j, (g.coeff x)⁻¹ i j *
    (coordDeriv i (coordDeriv j u) x - ∑ k, christoffel g x k i j * coordDeriv k u x)

/-- Riemannian length of a path on the unit parameter interval. -/
def pathLength {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (γ : ℝ → Space n) : ℝ :=
  ∫ t in (0 : ℝ)..1, Real.sqrt
    (∑ i, ∑ j, g.coeff (γ t) i j * deriv γ t i * deriv γ t j)

/-- Infimum of smooth path lengths, the intrinsic Riemannian distance. -/
def distance {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (x y : Space n) : ℝ :=
  sInf {L : ℝ | ∃ γ : ℝ → Space n,
    ContDiff ℝ 1 γ ∧ γ 0 = x ∧ γ 1 = y ∧ L = pathLength g γ}

/-- Sequential metric completeness, for the intrinsic distance, not the background one. -/
def Complete {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) : Prop :=
  ∀ z : ℕ → Space n,
    (∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ N : ℕ, ∀ p ≥ N, ∀ q ≥ N, distance g (z p) (z q) < ε) →
    ∃ x : Space n, ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ N : ℕ, ∀ p ≥ N, distance g (z p) x < ε

def EuclideanNearOrigin {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) : Prop :=
  ∃ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε ∧ ∀ x : Space n, (∑ i, x i ^ 2) < ε ^ 2 → g.coeff x = 1

/-- Riemannian measure of the intrinsic open ball, retaining infinite values. -/
def ballVolume {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (R : ℝ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  ∫⁻ x in {x : Space n | distance g 0 x < R},
    ENNReal.ofReal (Real.sqrt (g.coeff x).det) ∂volume

/-- The ordinary Euclidean unit ball volume, in Cartesian Lebesgue measure. -/
def omega (n : ℕ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  volume {x : Space n | (∑ i, x i ^ 2) < 1}

/-- The volume-ratio limit is finite, positive, and strictly below one. -/
def PositiveSubunitAVR {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) : Prop :=
  ∃ a : ℝ, 0 < a ∧ a < 1 ∧
    Tendsto (fun R : ℝ => ballVolume g R / (omega n * ENNReal.ofReal (R ^ n)))
      atTop (𝓝 (ENNReal.ofReal a))

/-- Membership in the actual space H_k, with the bound required at every point. -/
def HarmonicGrowth {n : ℕ} (g : SmoothMetric n) (k : ℕ) (u : Space n → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ContDiff ℝ ∞ u ∧ (∀ x, laplaceBeltrami g u x = 0) ∧
    ∃ C : ℝ, 0 ≤ C ∧ ∀ x, |u x| ≤ C * (1 + distance g 0 x) ^ k


def euclideanDimension (n k : ℕ) : ℕ :=
  (n + k - 1).choose k + (n + k - 2).choose (k - 1)

/-- The manuscript's unconditional main existence claim. -/
def MainClaim : Prop :=
  ∃ (n k : ℕ) (g : SmoothMetric n), Even n ∧ 8 ≤ n ∧ 2 ≤ k ∧
    Complete g ∧ RicciNonnegative g ∧ EuclideanNearOrigin g ∧ PositiveSubunitAVR g ∧
    ∃ u : Fin (euclideanDimension n k + 1) → (Space n → ℝ),
      LinearIndependent ℝ u ∧ ∀ i, HarmonicGrowth g k (u i)



axiom mainStatement : MainClaim

theorem main : MainClaim := mainStatement

end HarmonicCounterexample

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For some even <i>n</i> ≥ 8 and integer <i>k</i> ≥ 2, we construct a complete smooth metric on ℝ<sup><i>n</i></sup> with nonnegative Ricci curvature whose space of real harmonic functions of pointwise polynomial growth at most <i>k</i> has dimension larger than the Euclidean harmonic-polynomial dimension. The metric is Euclidean near the origin, has asymptotic volume ratio strictly between zero and one, and has nonunique tangent cones at infinity. This answers the integer-degree form of Yau's dimension comparison question in the negative.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a complete connected smooth Riemannian manifold $(M^n,g)$ without boundary, let $\mathcal H_d(M,g)$ be the real vector space of harmonic functions satisfying $$|u(x)|\leq C_u(1+d_g(o,x))^d\qquad(x\in M)$$ for some finite constant $C_u$, where $d\geq0$ and $o\in M$ is a base point. Changing $o$ does not change the space. Write $h_d(M,g)=\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathcal H_d(M,g)$. On Euclidean space, an entire harmonic function with integer growth at most $k$ is a harmonic polynomial of degree at most $k$. Consequently <a id="eq:euclidean-comparator"></a>$$\label{eq:euclidean-comparator}
 h_k(\mathbb R^n,g_{\mathrm E})
 =\binom{n+k-1}{k}+\binom{n+k-2}{k-1}\qquad(k\geq1).$$ The integer-degree comparison problem asks whether $\operatorname{Ric}_g
\geq0$ forces $h_k(M,g)\leq h_k(\mathbb R^n,g_{\mathrm E})$ for every integer $k\geq1$. The condition defining $\mathcal H_k$ is pointwise at every distance. Bounds only along a sequence of spheres do not establish it.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are an even integer $n\geq8$, an integer $k\geq2$, and a complete smooth metric $g$ on $\mathbb R^n$ such that $\operatorname{Ric}_g\geq0$ and $$h_k(\mathbb R^n,g)>h_k(\mathbb R^n,g_{\mathrm E}).$$ The metric is Euclidean near the origin, and its asymptotic volume ratio satisfies $$0<\lim_{R\to\infty}
 \frac{\operatorname{vol}_g B_g(0,R)}{\omega_nR^n}<1,$$ where $\omega_n$ is the volume of the Euclidean unit ball.*

</div>

The exact finite spectral selection in Appendix <a href="#sec:finite-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-selection">8</a> permits the choices $n=16$ and $k=50000$.

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> answers the integer-degree form of Yau’s dimension comparison question in the negative. Yau’s questions on polynomial-growth harmonic functions distinguish finite dimensionality from the sharper Euclidean comparison <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Yau](#ref-Yau), [LiTam](#ref-LiTam)]. Li and Tam proved the sharp linear-growth bound $h_1(M,g)\leq n+1$ under nonnegative Ricci curvature <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[LiTam](#ref-LiTam)]. Colding and Minicozzi proved finite dimensionality at every fixed degree and later obtained bounds of the optimal order $d^{n-1}$ as $d$ grows <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CM97](#ref-CM97), [CM98](#ref-CM98)]. Those estimates leave open the exact Euclidean count at a particular integer degree.

Donnelly constructed counterexamples to the analogous comparison at a real degree strictly between one and two in dimensions at least five <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Donnelly](#ref-Donnelly), [CaiLai](#ref-CaiLai)]. That fact alone does not decide the integer endpoint two: the Euclidean space of quadratic harmonic polynomials is larger than its space at a degree between one and two. Cai and Lai make this distinction explicit and establish sharp comparison with the additional hypothesis of local conformal flatness <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CaiLai](#ref-CaiLai)]. The theorem above addresses the integer question under the Ricci hypothesis alone.

Cone-spectral results explain another boundary of the problem. For complete manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature, maximal volume growth, and a unique tangent cone at infinity, Huang’s Theorem 1.6 relates the harmonic dimension to the spectrum of the cone link <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Huang](#ref-Huang)]. Our metric instead has round and nonround subsequential cone limits. Nonunique cones with positive Ricci curvature and Euclidean volume growth already occur in Perelman’s construction <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Perelman](#ref-Perelman)]; Colding and Naber developed a method for slowly varying link families with a common volume form <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ColdingNaber](#ref-ColdingNaber)]. We use the same geometric possibility of a moving link, while proving the curvature estimates for our particular deformations. Xu’s nonunique-cone three-circles theorem requires a comparison exponent outside the union of cone-degree spectra <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Xu](#ref-Xu), Theorem 3.4]. A separate geometric conclusion of our construction is that this union contains a neighborhood of the selected integer $k$, so the theorem cannot be applied at $k$ or at sufficiently nearby exponents.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Why a moving link can change the count

Put $n=m+1$. On the metric cone $dr^2+r^2h$ over an $m$-dimensional link, an angular eigenfunction of the nonnegative Laplacian with eigenvalue $\lambda$ has a growing homogeneous harmonic mode $r^d\phi$, where $$d(d+m-1)=\lambda,\qquad d\geq0.$$ For one fixed link, each angular direction therefore carries one fixed growth rate. Our construction lets a harmonic function use several rates in succession. The challenge is to arrange that succession exactly: an arbitrarily small component in an unwanted faster direction can dominate after a sufficiently long interval.

<a id="eq:overview-selection"></a>
The links are volume-normalized Berger metrics on odd spheres $S^m$, $m=2s-1$. Their distinguished Hopf direction is allowed to change. Differentiation along this circle direction is the Hopf generator; on complexified harmonics its eigenvalues are $\mathrm i$ times the integer Hopf charges. Every angular Laplacian nevertheless preserves the same finite-dimensional space $V_l$ of round spherical harmonics of degree $l$; write $N_l=\dim_{\mathbb R}V_l$. At a fixed nonround link, write its growing rates on $V_l$ in increasing order as $d_{l,1},\ldots,d_{l,N_l}$. The spectral calculation supplies a finite $L\geq2$ and integers $0\leq M_l\leq N_l$ for $2\leq l\leq L$ such that $$\label{eq:overview-selection}
 \sum_{l=2}^L M_l>h_k(\mathbb R^{m+1},g_{\mathrm E}),
 \qquad
 \frac1{M_l}\sum_{i=1}^{M_l}d_{l,i}<k\quad(M_l>0).$$ Thus the count will be large enough if each selected harmonic function can spend asymptotically equal amounts of logarithmic radius at all the selected rates in its block. The inequality for the mean is strict; individual rates may exceed $k$.

The source of <a href="#eq:overview-selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:overview-selection">[eq:overview-selection]</a> is the distribution of Hopf charges inside $V_l$. The squared charges divided by $l^2$ tend to a beta distribution. The mean of the lowest fraction of rates is described by an integral of its decreasing quantile. In a sufficiently large odd link dimension, a weighted quantile inequality shows that selecting directions by their mean produces a strict surplus over the Euclidean count. This fixes the link parameters, the integer $k$, and a finite degree range before the radial construction begins. An exact-integer computation gives a separate finite selection at specific parameters; its role is to verify that finite spectral input, rather than the subsequent geometric or transmission proof.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### From angular control to entire harmonic functions

Short deformations near a round link provide the exchanges of directions. For every fixed finite range $2\leq l\leq L$, the trace-free squares of the Hopf generators, using all orthogonal complex structures, generate $$\bigoplus_{l=2}^L\mathfrak{sl}(V_l).$$ The real form and the direct sum matter: one must choose the same finite sequence of geometric pulses while independently arranging a prescribed signed permutation in each $V_l$. A finite product of exponentials realizes these permutations at a parameter value where the word map has invertible differential. The transmission argument below shows that this algebraic freedom survives the harmonic equation.

Write the metric as $g=dr^2+r^2\gamma(t)$ with $t=\log r$. A harmonic function in $V_l$ is governed by a second-order matrix equation. Smoothness at the Euclidean center selects a distinguished regular solution space. Its logarithmic derivative $P_l$ satisfies a matrix Riccati equation; positive barriers keep $P_l$ bounded and symmetric. A long round rest makes $P_l$ approach a scalar matrix, independently of all earlier admissible pulses. During the next control interval, which is short relative to the following dwell, the determinant-normalized value transfer converges in $C^1$ to the finite algebraic word as the period index tends to infinity.

The pulse-end approximation is only an intermediate fact: the outgoing radial derivative can still inject a small component into a faster direction during the following long leg, on which the angular eigenvectors remain fixed. We factor out the positive diagonal transfer $D_{l,j}$ obtained by entering that entire leg with the round growing derivative. The remaining multiplier of the derivative error is bounded independently of the leg’s length and anisotropy (Lemma <a href="#lem:leg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:leg">14</a>). Thus the full-period map, with this diagonal factor removed and its determinant normalized, is $C^1$-close to the ideal word, uniformly over all preceding controls. Its local inverse tunes every complete period exactly to $s_{l,j}D_{l,j}\Pi_l$, where $\Pi_l$ cycles the selected axes and $s_{l,j}>0$ is a scalar.

The geometry and the growth argument use different features of the same schedule. In period $j$, the control intervals have length comparable to $j^6$ and the nonround dwell has length $j^7$, while the accumulated logarithmic radius is comparable to $j^8$. Angular motion is consequently slow enough for a small concave radial tail to preserve nonnegative Ricci curvature. The diagonal leg contributes $\log(D_{l,j})_{ii}=j^7(d_{l,i}+o(1))$, while $\log s_{l,j}=O(j^6)$. Exact cycling and the asymptotic equality of finitely many consecutive dwell lengths yield the means in <a href="#eq:overview-selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:overview-selection">[eq:overview-selection]</a>. Bounded Riccati matrices control the intervals between period endpoints. Since a whole period is negligible compared with the accumulated logarithmic radius, the same exponent bound holds at every radius. The strict inequality below $k$ then gives the pointwise growth condition, and invertibility of the regular fundamental matrices gives the claimed number of independent smooth harmonic functions.

All spaces and functions are real unless explicitly complexified. Angular Laplacians are nonnegative. Matrices act on column vectors, and positivity of a matrix means positivity as a symmetric operator. Constants may depend on the fixed finite degree range and fixed compact family of controls; they are uniform in the period index and in the preceding admissible controls.

Sections <a href="#sec:links" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:links">2</a> and <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">3</a> establish the Berger formulas and the finite spectral surplus. Section <a href="#sec:control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:control">4</a> proves simultaneous real control, and Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">5</a> realizes every admitted control history by a complete metric with nonnegative Ricci curvature. Section <a href="#sec:transmission" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transmission">6</a> tunes the full-period transmissions exactly; Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">7</a> proves the every-point growth estimate and the cone conclusions. Appendix <a href="#sec:finite-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-selection">8</a> records the independent finite arithmetic selection.

<a id="sec:links"></a>
### Volume-normalized Berger links

We need angular metrics that retain fixed finite-dimensional harmonic spaces while varying their spectra. This section derives their curvature and angular operators; the next section selects the directions that will be cycled.

Fix an odd integer $m=2s-1$, with $s\geq4$, and let $g_0$ be the unit round metric on $S^m\subset\mathbb R^{2s}$. An orthogonal complex structure is a real linear map $J$ satisfying $J^2=-I$ and $J^{\mathsf T}J=I$. It determines the unit round Killing field and its dual one-form $$\xi_J(x)=Jx,\qquad \eta_J=g_0(\xi_J,\cdot).$$ Write $$P_J=\xi_J\otimes\eta_J,\qquad D_Ju=du(\xi_J).$$ Thus $P_J$ is the projection onto the Hopf direction. For $a,q>0$, define $$\widetilde g_{q,J}=g_0+(q-1)\eta_J^2,\qquad
 G(a,q,J)=a^2q^{-1/m}\widetilde g_{q,J}.$$ The factor $q^{-1/m}$ makes the volume independent of $q$. All orthogonal complex structures will be allowed; we do not restrict them to one component of their parameter space.

This volume normalization and the corresponding Laplacian decomposition are classical; see Tanno <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Tanno](#ref-Tanno), Sections 2–4]. His parameter $t$ corresponds to $q=t^m$ when $a=1$. Classical curvature calculations for these spheres appear in Bourguignon–Karcher <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BourguignonKarcher](#ref-BourguignonKarcher), Section 6.6]. We record the formulas and their direct proofs in our parameters below.

For $l\geq0$, let $V_l$ be the real vector space of restrictions to $S^m$ of homogeneous harmonic polynomials of degree $l$ on $\mathbb R^{m+1}$. Equip $V_l$ with the round $L^2$ inner product and put $$N_l=\dim_{\mathbb R}V_l
     =\binom{m+l}{m}-\binom{m+l-2}{m},
 \qquad B_l=l(l+m-1),$$ where the second binomial coefficient is zero for $l<2$. For completeness, on homogeneous polynomials give distinct monomials orthogonal inner products with $\langle x^\alpha,x^\alpha\rangle=\alpha!$. The adjoint of the Euclidean polynomial Laplacian from degree $l$ to degree $l-2$ is multiplication by $|x|^2$, which is injective. The Laplacian is therefore surjective, giving the dimension formula. Restriction to the sphere is injective on homogeneous polynomials. Euclidean polar coordinates give $$\Delta_{g_0}^{+}\big|_{V_l}=B_lI,
 \qquad \Delta_h^{+}:=-\operatorname{div}_h\nabla_h.$$ Rotations preserve $V_l$. In particular, $D_J$ preserves $V_l$ and is skew-adjoint for its round inner product.

<a id="stmt-lem-berger-metrics"></a>
<div id="lem:berger-metrics" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:link-margin"></a>
<a id="eq:angular-operator"></a>
**Lemma 2**. *The volume form of $G(a,q,J)$ is $a^m\,d\operatorname{vol}_{g_0}$. Its Ricci endomorphism has eigenvalues $$a^{-2}q^{1/m}\bigl((m-1)-2(q-1)\bigr)
 \quad\hbox{horizontally},\qquad
 a^{-2}q^{1/m}(m-1)q
 \quad\hbox{vertically}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:link-margin}
 \operatorname{Ric}_{G(a,q,J)}>(m-1)G(a,q,J)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 a^2<q^{1/m}
       \min\left\{1-\frac{2(q-1)}{m-1},\,q\right\}.$$ Every $V_l$ is preserved by the positive Laplacian of $G(a,q,J)$, whose restriction is $$\label{eq:angular-operator}
 A_l(a,q,J)
 =a^{-2}q^{1/m}
       \left[B_lI+(q^{-1}-1)(-D_J^2|_{V_l})\right].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-berger-metrics"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-berger-metrics</summary>

*Proof.* We suppress $J$ from the notation. Relative to $g_0$, the metric $\widetilde g_q$ has eigenvalues $1$ on the $(m-1)$-dimensional horizontal space and $q$ on the Hopf direction. Its volume form is $\sqrt q\,d\operatorname{vol}_{g_0}$. Multiplication of the metric by $a^2q^{-1/m}$ multiplies this volume form by $a^m q^{-1/2}$, proving the volume assertion.

Here is a direct curvature calculation. Let $\nabla$ be the round connection and set $\phi X=\nabla_X\xi$. Tangent projection of the ambient derivative gives $$\phi X=JX+\eta(X)x,\qquad
 \phi^*=-\phi,\qquad \phi\xi=0,\qquad
 \phi^2=-I+\xi\otimes\eta,$$ and differentiation gives $$(\nabla_X\eta)(Y)=g_0(\phi X,Y),\qquad
 (\nabla_X\phi)Y=\eta(Y)X-g_0(X,Y)\xi.$$ Put $\delta=q-1$, which is constant on the sphere. If $C=\widetilde\nabla-\nabla$ is the difference between the connections of $\widetilde g_q$ and $g_0$, the Koszul formula yields $$\begin{aligned}
 2\widetilde g_q(C(X,Y),Z)
 &=(\nabla_X\widetilde g_q)(Y,Z)
   +(\nabla_Y\widetilde g_q)(X,Z)
   -(\nabla_Z\widetilde g_q)(X,Y)\\
 &=2\delta\left[\eta(X)g_0(\phi Y,Z)
                    +\eta(Y)g_0(\phi X,Z)\right].
\end{aligned}$$ The vectors $\phi X,\phi Y$ are horizontal, so $$C(X,Y)=\delta\bigl(\eta(X)\phi Y+\eta(Y)\phi X\bigr).$$ In particular, $\operatorname{tr}[X\mapsto C(X,Z)]=0$. Tracing the connection change formula for curvature therefore gives $$(\operatorname{Ric}_{\widetilde g_q}-\operatorname{Ric}_{g_0})(Y,Z)
 =\operatorname{tr}\bigl[X\mapsto(\nabla_XC)(Y,Z)\bigr]
  -\operatorname{tr}\bigl[X\mapsto C(Y,C(X,Z))\bigr].$$ These are endomorphism traces and can be computed in a round orthonormal frame. Using the identities for $\phi$, the four terms obtained by differentiating $C$ contribute, in order, $$-\delta(g_0-\eta^2),\quad
 \delta(m-1)\eta^2,\quad
 -\delta(g_0-\eta^2),\quad
 \delta(m-1)\eta^2.$$ Thus the first trace is $\delta(-2g_0+2m\eta^2)(Y,Z)$. For the second trace, one has $$C(Y,C(X,Z))
 =\delta^2\eta(Y)
   \bigl[-\eta(X)Z-\eta(Z)X+2\eta(X)\eta(Z)\xi\bigr],$$ whose trace in $X$ is $-(m-1)\delta^2\eta(Y)\eta(Z)$. Since $\operatorname{Ric}_{g_0}=(m-1)g_0$, this proves the tensor identity $$\operatorname{Ric}_{\widetilde g_q}
 =\bigl((m-1)-2(q-1)\bigr)(g_0-\eta^2)
   +(m-1)q^2\eta^2.$$ The squared length of $\xi$ in $\widetilde g_q$ is $q$. Hence the vertical eigenvalue of its Ricci endomorphism is $(m-1)q$, while the horizontal eigenvalue is $(m-1)-2(q-1)$. Constant metric scaling leaves the covariant Ricci tensor unchanged and divides its endomorphism eigenvalues by the scale. This proves the stated eigenvalues and <a href="#eq:link-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-margin">[eq:link-margin]</a>.

Finally, the inverse metric is $$G(a,q,J)^{-1}
 =a^{-2}q^{1/m}
     \left[g_0^{-1}+(q^{-1}-1)\xi\otimes\xi\right].$$ Because its volume form is a spatially constant multiple of round volume, its divergence on vector fields is the round divergence. The field $\xi$ has zero round divergence, so $\operatorname{div}_{g_0}((D_Ju)\xi)=D_J^2u$. It follows that $$\Delta_{G(a,q,J)}^{+}u
 =a^{-2}q^{1/m}
       \left[\Delta_{g_0}^{+}u+(q^{-1}-1)(-D_J^2u)\right].$$ Restricting this identity to $V_l$ gives <a href="#eq:angular-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-operator">[eq:angular-operator]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:counting"></a>
### A strict spectral surplus

Fix the standard orthogonal complex structure $J_0$ on $\mathbb R^{2s}$. For $a,q>0$, let $\lambda_{l,1}(a,q)\le\cdots\le\lambda_{l,N_l}(a,q)$ be the eigenvalues, counted with their real multiplicities, of the positive angular operator in <a href="#eq:angular-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-operator">[eq:angular-operator]</a> for $G(a,q,J_0)$. Define the corresponding nonnegative exponents by <a id="eq:dwell-exponents"></a>$$\label{eq:dwell-exponents}
 d_{l,i}(a,q)\bigl(d_{l,i}(a,q)+m-1\bigr)=\lambda_{l,i}(a,q),
 \qquad d_{l,i}(a,q)\ge0.$$ We will find more than the Euclidean number of directions whose exponents have mean strictly below a common integer. The subsequent construction will realize these means by cycling the selected directions.

The Hopf-charge decomposition underlying this calculation is described by Tanno <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Tanno](#ref-Tanno), Lemmas 3.1 and 4.1]; we prove the exact multiplicities and the distribution needed for the count.

<a id="stmt-lem-hopf-distribution"></a>
<div id="lem:hopf-distribution" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hopf-multiplicity"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Hopf-weight distribution). *For $m=2s-1$ and $0\le b\le l$, the eigenvalue $(2b-l)^2$ of $-D_{J_0}^2$ on $V_l$ has the following contribution to its multiplicity: $$\begin{aligned}
 h_{l,b}
 &=\binom{s+b-1}{s-1}\binom{s+l-b-1}{s-1}
   -\binom{s+b-2}{s-1}\binom{s+l-b-2}{s-1} \label{eq:hopf-multiplicity}\\
 &=\frac{l+s-1}{s-1}
   \binom{b+s-2}{s-2}\binom{l-b+s-2}{s-2}.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ Terms with a missing bidegree in the first line are zero. With probabilities $h_{l,b}/N_l$, the quantities $(2b-l)^2/l^2$ converge in distribution, as $l\to\infty$, to $$Y_m\sim\operatorname{Beta}\left(\frac12,\frac{m-1}{2}\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hopf-distribution"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hopf-distribution</summary>

*Proof.* Complexify $V_l$ and decompose homogeneous polynomials into bidegrees $(b,l-b)$ in $z_1,\ldots,z_s$ and their conjugates. The operator $D_{J_0}$ acts on this bidegree by $\mathrm i(2b-l)$. The contraction $\sum_j\partial_{z_j}\partial_{\bar z_j}$ maps onto the polynomials of bidegree $(b-1,l-b-1)$: in the positive monomial inner product in which $z^\alpha\bar z^\beta$ has squared norm $\alpha!\beta!$, its adjoint is multiplication by $\sum_j z_j\bar z_j$, which is injective. Taking the difference of dimensions gives the first line of <a href="#eq:hopf-multiplicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hopf-multiplicity">[eq:hopf-multiplicity]</a>; cancellation gives the second. The contributions indexed by $b$ and $l-b$ belong to the same eigenvalue of $-D_{J_0}^2$ and are added; the middle contribution is counted once. Since this operator is real symmetric, the complex dimension of each complexified eigenspace equals the real dimension of the corresponding original real eigenspace. There is no additional factor of two.

<a id="eq:harmonic-multiplicity-asymptotic"></a>
Summing the second line by the binomial convolution identity gives $$\label{eq:harmonic-multiplicity-asymptotic}
 N_l=\frac{l+s-1}{s-1}\binom{l+2s-3}{2s-3}
 =\binom{m+l}{m}-\binom{m+l-2}{m}
 \sim\frac{2}{(m-1)!}\,l^{m-1}.$$ More precisely, $h_{l,b}/N_l$ is a beta-binomial probability. If $X$ has density proportional to $[x(1-x)]^{s-2}$ on $(0,1)$ and, conditionally on $X$, $K_l$ is binomial with parameters $l,X$, then integration of the binomial probability against this density gives $\mathbb P(K_l=b)=h_{l,b}/N_l$. Since $$\mathbb E\left[\left(\frac{K_l}{l}-X\right)^2\right]
 =\frac{\mathbb E[X(1-X)]}{l}\le\frac{1}{4l},$$ we have $K_l/l\to X$ in probability in this representation. The change of variable $y=(2x-1)^2$ gives density proportional to $y^{-1/2}(1-y)^{s-2}$, proving the assertion. ◻

</details>

For $0<v<1$, define the upper-tail quantile $y_m(v)\in(0,1)$ by $$\mathbb P\bigl(Y_m>y_m(v)\bigr)=v,
 \qquad
 \mathcal J_m:=\int_0^1 y_m(v)\log(1/v)\,dv.$$ The threshold $2/(m-1)$ in the next lemma comes from the horizontal factor in the link curvature condition <a href="#eq:link-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-margin">[eq:link-margin]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-weighted-quantile"></a>
<div id="lem:weighted-quantile" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:weighted-quantile-gap"></a>
**Lemma 4** (A weighted quantile inequality). *For all sufficiently large odd $m$, $$\label{eq:weighted-quantile-gap}
 (m-1)\mathcal J_m>2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weighted-quantile"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-quantile</summary>

<a id="eq:normal-quantile-limit"></a>
*Proof.* Put $r=m-1$. The beta density, equivalently polar coordinates for independent standard normal variables, gives the representation $$rY_m\overset{\mathrm d}=\frac{Z^2}{Z^2/r+W_r/r},$$ where $Z$ is standard normal and $W_r$ is an independent sum of $r$ squared standard normal variables. Since $\mathbb E[W_r/r]=1$ and $\operatorname{Var}(W_r/r)=2/r$, we obtain $rY_m\Rightarrow Z^2$. The limiting distribution is continuous and strictly increasing on $(0,\infty)$, so its upper quantile $y_\infty(v)$ satisfies $r y_m(v)\to y_\infty(v)$ for every $0<v<1$. Fatou’s lemma therefore gives $$\label{eq:normal-quantile-limit}
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}(m-1)\mathcal J_m
 \ge \int_0^1 y_\infty(v)\log(1/v)\,dv
 =\mathbb E\left[R^2\log\frac{1}{Q(R)}\right],$$ where $R=|Z|$ and $Q(x)=\mathbb P(R>x)$. The last equality follows since $Q(R)$ is uniform on $(0,1)$. This expectation is finite: integration of the half-normal density on $[x,x+1]$ gives $Q(x)\ge\sqrt{2/\pi}\,e^{-(x+1)^2/2}$.

<a id="eq:normal-quantile-lower-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:strict-normal-constant"></a>
For $x>0$, integration of the same density gives the classical Mills upper bound (see <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Lee1992](#ref-Lee1992), Section 4, p. 113]) $$Q(x)=\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\int_x^\infty e^{-t^2/2}\,dt
 \le\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,\frac{e^{-x^2/2}}{x}.$$ Consequently, writing $A=\mathbb E[R^2\log R]$, we have $$\label{eq:normal-quantile-lower-bound}
 \mathbb E\left[R^2\log\frac1{Q(R)}\right]
 \ge\frac32+A+\frac12\log\frac\pi2.$$ Integration by parts against the half-normal density yields $\mathbb E[R^4\log R]=3A+1$. Under the probability measure $d\nu=R^4\,d\mathbb P/3$, Jensen’s inequality for the logarithm applied to $1/R$ now gives $$A+\frac13=\mathbb E_\nu[\log R]
 \ge-\log\mathbb E_\nu[1/R]
 =\log\frac3{\mathbb E[R^3]}
 =\log\frac3{2\sqrt{2/\pi}}.$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:normal-quantile-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-quantile-lower-bound">[eq:normal-quantile-lower-bound]</a> proves $$\label{eq:strict-normal-constant}
 \mathbb E\left[R^2\log\frac1{Q(R)}\right]
 \ge\frac76+\log\frac{3\pi}{4}>2.$$ Here the final strict inequality can be checked without decimal approximations. Indeed, $\arctan x>x-x^3/3$ for $x>0$ gives $$\frac\pi4=\arctan\frac12+\arctan\frac13
 >\frac{505}{648}>\frac79,$$ so $3\pi/4>7/3$. Strict convexity of $t\mapsto1/t$ gives the midpoint bounds $$\log\frac73
 =\int_1^{5/3}\frac{dt}{t}+\int_{5/3}^{7/3}\frac{dt}{t}
 >\frac{2/3}{4/3}+\frac{2/3}{2}=\frac56.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:normal-quantile-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-quantile-limit">[eq:normal-quantile-limit]</a> and <a href="#eq:strict-normal-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-normal-constant">[eq:strict-normal-constant]</a> imply <a href="#eq:weighted-quantile-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-quantile-gap">[eq:weighted-quantile-gap]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:cutoff-smallness"></a>
Before selecting the link, fix a smooth nondecreasing cutoff $\chi$, zero near $( -\infty,2]$ and one near $[4,\infty)$, and put $$I_\chi=\int_2^\infty\chi(t)(1+t)^{-5/4}\,dt>0.$$ The radial construction will require only the extra smallness condition $$\label{eq:cutoff-smallness}
 \frac{-\log a_*}{I_\chi}\,
 \sup_{t>2}\chi(t)(1+t)^{-5/4}\leq\frac14.$$ It depends on the fixed cutoff and link scale, before any finite degree range or control family has been chosen. The freedom in the next lemma allows this condition to be imposed at the outset.

<a id="stmt-lem-spectral-surplus"></a>
<div id="lem:spectral-surplus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:selection"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Spectral surplus). *There is an odd $m=2s-1\ge7$ for which the following choices are possible. The parameters $a_*\in(0,1)$ and $q_*>1$ can be taken arbitrarily close to $1$, with <a href="#eq:link-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-margin">[eq:link-margin]</a> holding at $a=a_*$ for every $q\in[1,q_*]$. For these parameters there are integers $k\ge2$, $L\ge2$, and $0\le M_l\le N_l$ for $2\le l\le L$ such that, on abbreviating $d_{l,i}=d_{l,i}(a_*,q_*)$, $$\label{eq:selection}
 \overline d_l:=\frac1{M_l}\sum_{i=1}^{M_l}d_{l,i}<k
 \quad\text{whenever }M_l>0,
 \qquad
 \sum_{l=2}^L M_l>\sum_{l=0}^k N_l.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spectral-surplus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spectral-surplus</summary>

*Proof.* **Choosing the link.** Choose an odd $m\ge7$ satisfying Lemma <a href="#lem:weighted-quantile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weighted-quantile">4</a>, and fix $$\frac2{m-1}<c<\mathcal J_m.$$ For a small positive parameter $\varepsilon$, set <a id="eq:dwell-parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:dwell-parameters}
 q_*=1+\varepsilon,
 \qquad \alpha_*^2=1-c\varepsilon,
 \qquad a_*^2=q_*^{1/m}\alpha_*^2.$$ For $q\ge1$, the right-hand side of <a href="#eq:link-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-margin">[eq:link-margin]</a> is $$F(q)=q^{1/m}\left(1-\frac{2(q-1)}{m-1}\right),
 \qquad
 F'(q)=\frac{m+1}{m(m-1)}q^{1/m-1}(1-2q)<0.$$ At $q=q_*$, the required strict inequality follows from $c>2/(m-1)$; it consequently holds throughout $[1,q_*]$. In particular $a_*^2<F(q_*)<1$.

<a id="eq:limiting-exponent-quantile"></a>
<a id="eq:limiting-mean-exponents"></a>
**Limiting means.** By <a href="#eq:angular-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-operator">[eq:angular-operator]</a>, the eigenvalues at the dwell parameters are the numbers $$\alpha_*^{-2}\left[B_l-
       \left(1-\frac1{q_*}\right)(2b-l)^2\right]$$ with the multiplicities in Lemma <a href="#lem:hopf-distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hopf-distribution">3</a>. Thus their increasing order corresponds to the decreasing order of the Hopf weights $(2b-l)^2$. If $d(d+m-1)=\lambda$ and $d\ge0$, then $0\le\sqrt\lambda-d\le(m-1)/2$. Since $B_l/l^2\to1$, Lemma <a href="#lem:hopf-distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hopf-distribution">3</a> and convergence of quantiles to a continuous, strictly increasing distribution imply $$\label{eq:limiting-exponent-quantile}
 \frac{d_{l,\lceil vN_l\rceil}}{l}\longrightarrow
 f_\varepsilon(v):=(1-c\varepsilon)^{-1/2}
 \sqrt{1-\left(1-\frac1{q_*}\right)y_m(v)}
 \qquad(0<v<1).$$ These rescaled exponents are uniformly bounded, so averaging the first $\lceil uN_l\rceil$ terms gives, for every $0<u<1$, $$\label{eq:limiting-mean-exponents}
 \frac1{l\lceil uN_l\rceil}
       \sum_{i=1}^{\lceil uN_l\rceil}d_{l,i}
 \longrightarrow
 g_\varepsilon(u):=\frac1u\int_0^u f_\varepsilon(v)\,dv.$$ One may see this directly by integrating the empirical quantile in <a href="#eq:limiting-exponent-quantile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-exponent-quantile">[eq:limiting-exponent-quantile]</a>; the possible last fractional term has size $O(N_l^{-1})$ after division by $l$.

Define $$I(\varepsilon):=\int_0^1 g_\varepsilon(u)^{-m}\,du.$$ All functions here are bounded away from zero for sufficiently small $\varepsilon$. Uniformly for $0<v<1$, $$f_\varepsilon(v)
 =1+\frac\varepsilon2\bigl(c-y_m(v)\bigr)+O(\varepsilon^2).$$ The same uniform remainder remains valid after averaging over $(0,u)$. Expansion of $g_\varepsilon(u)^{-m}$ and integration therefore give <a id="eq:counting-first-variation"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 I(\varepsilon)
 &=1+\frac{m\varepsilon}{2}
   \left[\int_0^1\frac1u\int_0^u y_m(v)\,dv\,du-c\right]
   +O(\varepsilon^2)\notag\\
 &=1+\frac{m\varepsilon}{2}(\mathcal J_m-c)
   +O(\varepsilon^2)>1
 \qquad\text{for all sufficiently small }\varepsilon>0.
 \label{eq:counting-first-variation}
\end{aligned}$$ The second equality uses the nonnegative integral identity $\int_v^1du/u=\log(1/v)$. Fix such an $\varepsilon$, as small as desired, from now on.

<a id="eq:counting-layer-cake"></a>
**Counting at integer thresholds.** For each positive integer $k$ and $l\ge2$, let $M_l(k)$ be the largest initial group of the ordered exponents whose mean is strictly less than $k$, taking $M_l(k)=0$ if no group qualifies. Only finitely many degrees contribute: the displayed eigenvalue formula bounds all $\lambda_{l,i}$ below by a positive constant times $l^2$, so $\min_i d_{l,i}$ grows at least linearly in $l$. The initial-group means are nondecreasing, and hence $$\label{eq:counting-layer-cake}
 M_l(k)=N_l\int_0^1
 \mathbf 1\left\{
   \frac1{\lceil uN_l\rceil}
   \sum_{i=1}^{\lceil uN_l\rceil}d_{l,i}<k
 \right\}\,du.$$ For fixed $u\in(0,1)$ and $\delta>0$, <a href="#eq:limiting-mean-exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-mean-exponents">[eq:limiting-mean-exponents]</a> bounds the mean in this indicator by $l(g_\varepsilon(u)+\delta/2)$ for every sufficiently large $l$. It is therefore strictly below $k$ whenever $l\le k/(g_\varepsilon(u)+\delta)$, including at the upper endpoint. Using <a href="#eq:harmonic-multiplicity-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:harmonic-multiplicity-asymptotic">[eq:harmonic-multiplicity-asymptotic]</a>, the sum of the corresponding $N_l$, divided by $\sum_{l=0}^kN_l$, has limit $(g_\varepsilon(u)+\delta)^{-m}$ as the integer $k\to\infty$. Letting $\delta\downarrow0$ and applying Fatou’s lemma to <a href="#eq:counting-layer-cake" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-layer-cake">[eq:counting-layer-cake]</a> yields <a id="eq:counting-liminf"></a>$$\label{eq:counting-liminf}
 \liminf_{\substack{k\to\infty\\ k\in\mathbb N}}
 \frac{\sum_{l=2}^\infty M_l(k)}{\sum_{l=0}^kN_l}
 \ge\int_0^1g_\varepsilon(u)^{-m}\,du
 =I(\varepsilon)>1.$$ Discarding the finitely many lower degrees in this argument changes no limit. Consequently every sufficiently large integer $k$ has a strict surplus. Taking $L$ to be the largest degree with $M_l(k)>0$ and setting $M_l=M_l(k)$ proves <a href="#eq:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection">[eq:selection]</a>. ◻

</details>

A selected prefix may be empty, a singleton, or all of $V_l$, and may cut a repeated real eigenspace. No gap at its last eigenvalue is required: choose the indicated number of real orthonormal eigenvectors. The selected span need not be invariant under any individual $D_J$; the full fixed space $V_l$ remains invariant under every angular operator.

Finally, the sum appearing in <a href="#eq:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection">[eq:selection]</a> is exactly the Euclidean comparison dimension in ambient dimension $n=m+1$: $$\sum_{l=0}^kN_l
 =\binom{m+k}{k}+\binom{m+k-1}{k-1}
 =h_k(\mathbb R^{m+1}).$$ To justify the Euclidean identification directly, let $u$ be a Euclidean harmonic function of growth at most the integer $k$. A smooth radial kernel $\psi_R(x)=R^{-(m+1)}\psi(x/R)$, supported in the ball of radius $R$ and with integral $1$, reproduces $u$ by the mean-value property. For each multi-index $\alpha$ of order $k+1$, moving derivatives onto the kernel gives, at any fixed $x$ and for $R\ge1$, $$|\partial^\alpha u(x)|
 \le \sup_{|y-x|\le R}|u(y)|\,
       \|\partial^\alpha\psi_R\|_{L^1}
 \le C_x R^kR^{-(k+1)}\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $u$ is a polynomial of degree at most $k$, and its homogeneous components are harmonic. The converse follows from homogeneity.

For the rest of the construction, the spectral data mean an odd $m=2s-1$ with $s\geq4$, a fixed cutoff $\chi$, and $0<a_*<1<q_*$ satisfying <a href="#eq:link-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-margin">[eq:link-margin]</a> throughout $[1,q_*]$ and <a href="#eq:cutoff-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cutoff-smallness">[eq:cutoff-smallness]</a>, together with finite $k,L\geq2$ and $0\leq M_l\leq N_l$ satisfying <a href="#eq:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection">[eq:selection]</a>. These are precisely the numerical hypotheses used in Sections <a href="#sec:control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:control">4</a>–<a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">7</a>; no later step uses the asymptotic route by which the data were obtained.

The choices in Lemma <a href="#lem:spectral-surplus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spectral-surplus">5</a> are made in the order $m,c,\varepsilon,k,L$. In particular any further requirement that $a_*$ be close to $1$ can be imposed when choosing $\varepsilon$, before the integer cutoff is fixed.

<a id="sec:control"></a>
### Control of the angular modes

The selections in Lemma <a href="#lem:spectral-surplus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spectral-surplus">5</a> will be mixed by cycling their basis directions. We first prove that the angular operators furnish the required finite-dimensional control. Throughout this section, $m=2s-1$, $s\geq4$, and $2\leq l\leq L$. We equip each real space $V_l$ of round spherical harmonics with its round $L^2$ inner product and write $N_l=\dim_{\mathbb R}V_l$. For an endomorphism $A$ of $V_l$, put $$A_{\mathrm{tf}}=A-\frac{\operatorname{tr}A}{N_l}I.$$ For every orthogonal complex structure $J$ on $\mathbb R^{2s}$, the operator $D_Ju(x)=du_x(Jx)$ preserves $V_l$. It is skew-adjoint, because its flow consists of round isometries. In particular, $(D_J^2)_{\mathrm{tf}}$ is a real symmetric trace-free operator.

<a id="stmt-prop-control"></a>
<div id="prop:control" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:control-generators"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Simultaneous control). *Fix positive numbers $\kappa_l$, $2\leq l\leq L$. As $J$ ranges over all orthogonal complex structures on $\mathbb R^{2s}$, the tuples $$X_J=\bigl(\kappa_l(D_J^2|_{V_l})_{\mathrm{tf}}\bigr)_{l=2}^L
 \label{eq:control-generators}$$ generate, under real linear combinations and brackets, the full Lie algebra $$\bigoplus_{l=2}^L\mathfrak{sl}(V_l).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-control"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-control</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove the assertion in a single degree $l$. The nonzero factor $\kappa_l$ has no effect on this assertion. Let $\mathfrak a\subset\mathfrak{sl}(V_l)$ be the real Lie algebra generated by $(D_J^2)_{\mathrm{tf}}$, and set $$W=V_l\otimes_{\mathbb R}\mathbb C,\qquad
 \mathfrak a_{\mathbb C}=\mathfrak a\otimes_{\mathbb R}\mathbb C.$$ Transpose on $W$ will always refer to the complex bilinear extension of the real round inner product. The single-degree argument first produces a rank-one symmetric endomorphism of $W$. Round conjugation and brackets then produce all trace-free endomorphisms of $W$. Finally, we separate the different degrees to obtain simultaneous control on their direct sum.

**Diagonal operators and individual weight blocks.** Choose orthonormal coordinates $(x_1,y_1,\ldots,x_s,y_s)$ and write $z_i=x_i+\mathrm i y_i$. For $\theta\in\mathbb R^s$, let $R_\theta$ rotate coordinate plane $i$ by $\theta_i$, and let $T_\theta u(x)=u(R_\theta x)$. The *weight space* of $\nu\in\mathbb Z^s$ is the character eigenspace $$W_\nu=\{u\in W:T_\theta u=e^{\mathrm i\sum_i\nu_i\theta_i}u
                    \text{ for every }\theta\}.$$ Thus $z_i$ has weight $e_i$, $\bar z_i$ has weight $-e_i$, and $D_{J_0}$ acts on $W_\nu$ by $\mathrm i\sum_i\nu_i$. In particular, a monomial of holomorphic degree $b$ and antiholomorphic degree $l-b$ has Hopf charge $2b-l$, consistently with Section <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">3</a>. Every occurring weight satisfies $$\sum_{i=1}^s|\nu_i|\leq l,\qquad
 l-\sum_{i=1}^s|\nu_i|\in2\mathbb Z.$$ Every weight on the extreme layer $$\Lambda_l=\left\{\nu\in\mathbb Z^s:
                    \sum_{i=1}^s|\nu_i|=l\right\}$$ occurs and has a one-dimensional weight space: it is spanned by the monomial using $z_i^{\nu_i}$ when $\nu_i\geq0$ and $\bar z_i^{-\nu_i}$ when $\nu_i<0$. This monomial is harmonic, since in each coordinate plane it involves at most one of $z_i,\bar
z_i$. It is the only degree-$l$ monomial of that weight.

More generally, every weight satisfying the displayed bound and parity condition occurs. To check its multiplicity, set $a=(l-\sum_i|\nu_i|)/2$, a nonnegative integer. Every monomial of weight $\nu$ is the corresponding signed monomial of degree $\sum_i|\nu_i|$, multiplied by $\prod_i(z_i\bar z_i)^{a_i}$ with $a_i\ge0$ and $\sum_i a_i=a$. The full polynomial weight space therefore has dimension $\binom{a+s-1}{s-1}$. The polynomial Laplacian preserves the weight and maps onto the corresponding weight space two degrees lower: its adjoint in the positive monomial inner product is a nonzero scalar multiple of multiplication by $\sum_i z_i\bar z_i$, which preserves weight and is injective. Thus the harmonic weight multiplicity is $$\binom{a+s-1}{s-1}-\binom{a+s-2}{s-1}
 =\binom{a+s-2}{s-2},$$ where the second term is zero when $a=0$. This standard weight formula also appears in Lauret–Miatello–Rossetti <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[LauretMiatelloRossetti](#ref-LauretMiatelloRossetti), Lemma 3.6]. In particular, interior weight spaces may have dimension greater than one. The interpolation below isolates an entire source-to-target weight block; we use a rank-one conclusion only when both endpoints are on the extreme layer.

Conjugating by a round rotation sends the family of generators to itself. Thus $\mathfrak a_{\mathbb C}$ is invariant under the round torus and contains each torus Fourier component of any of its elements. Indeed, Fourier projection is an integral of scalar multiples of torus conjugates, and a finite-dimensional vector subspace is closed.

For $\sigma=(\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_s)\in\{\pm1\}^s$, let $J_\sigma$ rotate coordinate plane $i$ with sign $\sigma_i$. On the $\nu$-weight space, $D_{J_\sigma}^2$ acts by $-(\sum_i\sigma_i\nu_i)^2$. For $i\ne j$, the identity $$2^{-s}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm1\}^s}
 \sigma_i\sigma_j\left[-\left(\sum_a\sigma_a\nu_a\right)^2\right]
 =-2\nu_i\nu_j$$ shows that $\mathfrak a_{\mathbb C}$ contains a diagonal operator $A_{ij}$ acting on weight $\nu$ by $\nu_i\nu_j+c_{ij}$, where $c_{ij}$ is independent of $\nu$. The scalar term has no effect on brackets.

<a id="eq:control-block-eigenvalues"></a>
An endomorphism Fourier component of *weight shift* $\delta$ satisfies $T_\theta A T_\theta^{-1}
=e^{\mathrm i\sum_i\delta_i\theta_i}A$; equivalently, it maps each $W_\nu$ into $W_{\nu+\delta}$, with absent weight spaces understood to be zero. On its block from $W_\nu$ to $W_{\nu+\delta}$, the operator $\operatorname{ad}A_{ij}$ acts by $$\nu_i\delta_j+\nu_j\delta_i+\delta_i\delta_j.
 \label{eq:control-block-eigenvalues}$$ If $\delta$ has at least three nonzero coordinates, these joint eigenvalues distinguish all source weights. In fact, equality for two sources, with difference $v$, implies $$v_i\delta_j+v_j\delta_i=0\qquad(i\ne j).$$ On the support of $\delta$, the ratios $v_i/\delta_i$ have pairwise sums zero; three such coordinates force every ratio to be zero. Pairing any coordinate outside the support with one in the support then gives $v_i=0$ there as well.

There are only finitely many weights. Polynomials in the commuting operators $\operatorname{ad}A_{ij}$ therefore project onto an individual source block within a fixed shift component. More explicitly, for each competing source choose one coordinate of <a href="#eq:control-block-eigenvalues" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:control-block-eigenvalues">[eq:control-block-eigenvalues]</a> that distinguishes it from the desired source, and multiply the corresponding linear interpolation factors. These operations preserve $\mathfrak a_{\mathbb C}$. When source and target belong to $\Lambda_l$, the isolated nonzero block is a rank-one directed matrix.

**A rank-one symmetric operator.** Regard an extreme weight as $l$ signed tokens, with no opposite signs at the same coordinate. We claim that the algebra contains the directed matrices for every legal move $$\text{two equal-sign tokens at one coordinate}
 \quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{one token at each of two other coordinates},$$ where the two latter signs are arbitrary and both endpoints must remain in $\Lambda_l$. Such a shift has three nonzero coordinates. It remains to exhibit a generator with a nonzero block for the move.

Here is an explicit simultaneous choice of the needed coefficients. For three distinct coordinate indices $i,j,k$, let $X_a,Y_a$ denote their real coordinate vectors and set $$JX_i=X_j,\qquad JY_i=X_k,\qquad JY_j=Y_k,$$ with the three reverse images determined by $J^2=-I$. Use the standard complex structure on the remaining coordinate planes. This defines an orthogonal complex structure. For signed variables $u_a^\epsilon=x_a+\mathrm i\epsilon y_a$, $\epsilon\in\{\pm1\}$, its vector field satisfies $$\begin{split}
 D_Ju_i^\epsilon&=-x_j-\mathrm i\epsilon x_k,\\
 D_Ju_j^\beta&=x_i-\mathrm i\beta y_k,\\
 D_Ju_k^\gamma&=y_i+\mathrm i\gamma y_j.
 \end{split}$$ For every choice of the three signs, the relevant replacement coefficients are $$\begin{array}{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}
 u_i^\epsilon\longmapsto u_j^\beta&
 u_i^\epsilon\longmapsto u_k^\gamma&
 u_j^\beta\longmapsto u_i^\epsilon&
 u_k^\gamma\longmapsto u_i^\epsilon
 \\[3pt]
 -\frac12&-\frac{\mathrm i\epsilon}{2}&
 \frac12&-\frac{\mathrm i\epsilon}{2}
 \end{array}$$ For completeness, there is no cancellation with other terms of $D_J^2$. In the independent complex linear coordinates $z_a,\bar z_a$, the identity $D_J^2u=-u$ for linear functions gives, on homogeneous degree-$l$ polynomials, $$D_J^2p=-lp+
